Towards Term-based Verification of Diagrammatic Equivalence
Julie Cailler, Noé Delorme, Simon Perdrix, Sophie Tourret

Abstract
A string diagram is a two-dimensional graphical representation that can be described as a one-dimensional term generated from a set of primitives using sequential and parallel compositions. Since different syntactic terms may represent the same diagram, this syntax is quotiented by a collection of coherence equations expressing equivalence up to deformation. This work lays foundations for automated reasoning about diagrammatic equivalence, motivated primarily by the verification of quantum circuit equivalences. We consider two classes of diagrams, for which we introduce normalizing term rewriting systems that equate diagrammatically equivalent terms. In both cases, we prove termination and confluence with the help of the proof assistant Isabelle/HOL.



1
Introduction

Diagrammatic reasoning has proved to be a powerful and expressive framework across a wide range of domains, including quantum computing
[
12
,
17
,
15
,
3
,
1
,
9
]
, linear algebra
[
21
,
5
]
, boolean circuits
[
10
]
, and natural language processing
[
11
,
24
]
. By representing computations as compositional diagrams in two dimensions, these formalisms offer intuitive and structural insights that are difficult to witness otherwise. As diagrammatic methods continue to improve, the need for automated reasoning techniques expands. In particular, problems such as equivalence checking, verification, certification, and diagrammatic manipulation require algorithmic foundations in order to scale.

An important motivation for our work arises from quantum computing, a field in which diagrammatic reasoning has been extensively developed
[
9
,
8
,
7
,
13
]
. In particular, quantum circuits admit natural diagrammatic representations, and reasoning about their equivalence is essential for tasks such as optimization, compilation, and verification. Our work is motivated by the goal of obtaining semi-automated verification techniques for quantum circuit equivalences. In this paper, we establish foundational results, in the form of normal forms and verified rewriting systems, that constitute essential building blocks towards full-fledged quantum circuit verification.

One way to represent diagrammatic reasoning problems suitably for automation is to adopt a syntactic term-based representation of diagrams. By encoding diagrams as terms equipped with operations for sequential and parallel compositions, diagrammatic deformations can be translated into equations between terms. This enables the use of term rewriting to prove diagram equivalences, including standard techniques for proving termination and confluence, for which there exists automated tools
[
14
,
23
,
6
,
25
]
. As a result, the term-based representation provides a natural framework for certifying diagrammatic equivalence in a rigorous and machine-checkable way.

The natural mathematical framework to encode diagrams as terms is monoidal categories, and more precisely PROs and PROPs
[
20
]
. Both structures are similar, with one distinction: a PROP additionally allows swapping wires. Formally, the terms are freely generated from a set of generators—corresponding to black box processes with a certain amount of input and output wires—and can be composed in two ways: in sequence with the operator
\fatsemi
and in parallel with the operator
\otimes
. Depending on the order in which the generators are syntactically composed, multiple terms may represent the same diagrammatic evolution. For instance,
(u_{1}\fatsemi u_{2})\otimes(v_{1}\fatsemi v_{2})
and
(u_{1}\otimes v_{1})\fatsemi(u_{2}\otimes v_{2})
can both be graphically depicted as follows.

u_{1}
u_{2}
v_{1}
v_{2}

Hence, the syntax of terms is quotiented by a collection of
coherence equations
capturing the intuitive notion of diagram equivalence. These equations can be turned into rewrite rules, allowing the rewrite of equivalent terms.

Contributions.

We focus on two diagrammatic equivalence problems. In the first one, we consider arbitrary state-and-effect-free PROs, meaning that all generators have at least one input wire and one output wire. We define a normal form together with an associated normalizing rewriting system, and prove termination and confluence. Then, any pair of diagrammatically equivalent terms can be rewritten one into the other using the normalization procedure. In the second problem, we consider the PRO of permutations, which is precisely the PROP with no generators. Building upon our previous normal form, we define a canonical form and a canonizing rewriting system that we also prove to be terminating and confluent. This ensures the existence and uniqueness of the canonical form for any permutation. In both cases, the key steps of the termination and confluence proofs are verified using the proof assistant Isabelle/HOL.

Related work.

Diagrammatic equivalence has also been studied with a fundamentally different approach: instead of working with a syntactic term representation, diagrams are encoded as hypergraphs and rewriting is studied at the level of graph structures
[
4
]
. Then, diagrammatic equivalence is witnessed by hypergraph isomorphism. In contrast, our work remains entirely in the term-based setting and directly equips terms with rewriting systems yielding explicit normal forms. Related questions have also been investigated in the context of the ZX-calculus, notably with the tool VyZX
[
19
]
. While sharing the same overall goal of certifying diagrammatic equivalences, their approach focuses on filling the gap between the use of inductive datatypes in proof assistants and the use of graph representation in ZX-calculus. Diagrammatic equivalence in the PRO of permutations has already been addressed, but from a diagrammatic perspective, where equivalence is handled implicitly via graphical transformation
[
18
]
. Although the resulting normal form is closely related in shape to ours, this work remains purely graphical and does not account for the syntactic order of sequential and parallel compositions.

Outline of the paper.

In
Section

2
, we fix the notations and give term-based definitions of PROs and PROPs. In
Section

3
, we introduce the normal form for an arbitrary state-and-effect-free PROP and prove the termination and confluence of its underlying term rewriting system. In
Section

4
, we introduce the canonical form for the PRO of permutations and prove the termination and confluence of its underlying term rewriting system. In
Section

5
, we provide our concluding remarks.

2
Background

The standard mathematical framework for diagrammatic reasoning is the one of
monoidal categories
. In particular, circuit-based graphical languages—such as quantum circuits—are defined as a particular kind of monoidal categories, namely PROs or PROPs (which stands for
PROduct and Permutation categories
). A PRO is a strict monoidal category generated by a single object, and a PROP is additionally symmetric (meaning that there are symmetry primitive allowing to swap wires). For non-category theorists, we provide self-contained definitions of PROs and PROPs in this section. Roughly speaking, these structures are generated from a set of generators—corresponding to basic gates—via sequential and parallel composition. Graphically, this amounts to represent the generators as boxes and connect them with wires.

2.1
Terms and their Diagrammatic Representation

Let
\Sigma
be a set of generators equipped with two functions
\textup{dom},\textup{cod}:\Sigma\to\mathbb{N}
. We inductively define the set of terms
\langle\Sigma\rangle
induced by
\Sigma
, and extend
\textup{dom},\textup{cod}:\langle\Sigma\rangle\to\mathbb{N}
as follows, using the notation
t:n\to m\in\langle\Sigma\rangle
(or simply
t:n\to m
if
\Sigma
is clear from the context) when
t\in\langle\Sigma\rangle
,
\textup{dom}(t)=n
and
\textup{cod}(t)=m
.

\displaystyle g:\textup{dom}(g)\to\textup{cod}(g)\in\langle\Sigma\rangle g\in\Sigma\hskip 30.00005pt\textup{id}_{n}:n\to n\in\langle\Sigma\rangle n\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle(u\fatsemi v):n\to m\in\langle\Sigma\rangle\lx@proof@logical@and u:n\to p\in\langle\Sigma\rangle v:p\to m\in\langle\Sigma\rangle\hskip 30.00005pt(u\otimes v):n+p\to m+q\in\langle\Sigma\rangle\lx@proof@logical@and u:n\to m\in\langle\Sigma\rangle v:p\to q\in\langle\Sigma\rangle

The
domain
and
codomain
of a term
t\in\langle\Sigma\rangle
are given by
\textup{dom}(t)
and
\textup{cod}(t)
, representing its number of inputs and outputs, respectively. We define the
defect
of
t
by
\textup{def}(t)\coloneqq\textup{dom}(t)-\textup{cod}(t)
. A term
t
is called a
state
if
\textup{dom}(t)=0
, and an
effect
if
\textup{cod}(t)=0
. Moreover, for each
t\in\langle\Sigma\rangle
, let
\textup{gen}(t)\subseteq\Sigma
denote the set of generators appearing in
t
. This set is defined inductively by
\textup{gen}(\textup{id}_{n})=\varnothing
,
\textup{gen}(g)=\{g\}
for
g\in\Sigma
, and
\textup{gen}(u\fatsemi v)\coloneqq\textup{gen}(u\otimes v)\coloneqq\textup{gen}(u)\cup\textup{gen}(v)
.

A common set of generators is the set of symmetries
\Sigma_{0}\coloneqq\{\sigma_{n,m}:n,m\in\mathbb{N}\}
with
\textup{dom}(\sigma_{n,m})=\textup{cod}(\sigma_{n,m})=n+m
. Intuitively,
\sigma_{n,m}
represents a diagram that exchanges two registers consisting of
n
and
m
wires, respectively. Terms admit a natural graphical representation as string diagrams. A term
t:n\to m
is depicted as a box with
n
input wires and
m
output wires; the identity
\textup{id}_{n}
is depicted as
n
parallel wires; and the symmetry
\sigma_{n,m}
is depicted as a swap of the
n
and
m
wires. Here are some example.

Moreover, given
r,s\in\mathbb{N}
, the set of contextualized terms
\langle\Sigma\rangle_{r,s}
is defined as the terms containing a single occurrence of an additional generator
[\cdot]:r\to s
called a
hole
. Then, given a contextualized term
K[\cdot]:n\to m\in\langle\Sigma\rangle_{r,s}
and a term
t:r\to s\in\langle\Sigma\rangle
, the substitution
K[t]:n\to m\in\langle\Sigma\rangle
is the term where the unique hole
[\cdot]
in
K[\cdot]
as been replaced by
t
.

2.2
Diagrammatic Equivalence

Up to here, terms are purely syntactic, and two terms are only equal if they are constructed exactly in the same way. However, several terms may be represented by the same diagram. Thus, we need the equivalence equations known as the
coherence equations
, depicted in
Figure

1
, to precisely capture the intuitive notion of diagram deformation, which yields our definition of PROs and PROPs.

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\textup{id}_{n}\fatsemi t&=&t&=&t\fatsemi\textup{id}_{m}\\[3.99994pt]
\scalebox{0.64}{{
\hbox to61.46pt{\vbox to22.34pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.28297pt\lower-11.16978pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{
{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -24.61 0 L 59.06 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -14.76 14.76 L 19.69 14.76}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 14.76 L 19.69 -14.76}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {

(10)

Figure 1
:

Equations

1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
and
6
are the coherence equations of PROs.
Equations

1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
and
10
are the coherence equations of PROPs. Dotted boxes and wires highlight the correspondence with the syntax. For convenience, we can draw a single wire to represent multiple wires when it is clear from the context.

The PRO freely generated by the set of generators
\Sigma
, denoted
\textbf{{PRO}}_{\Sigma}
, is defined as the collection of equivalence classes of terms
\langle\Sigma\rangle
under
Equations

1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
and
6
. Furthermore, given a set of equations
\mathcal{E}
between terms, let
\textbf{{PRO}}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{E}}
be the PRO whose equivalence classes are the ones of
\textbf{{PRO}}_{\Sigma}
additionally quotiented by the equations of
\mathcal{E}
. The equivalence relation is denoted
\textbf{{PRO}}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{E}}\vdash t_{1}=t_{2}
when
t_{1}
can be rewritten into
t_{2}
using
Equations

1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
and
6
together with the equations in
\mathcal{E}
. The equivalence relation is closed under context, meaning that if
\textbf{{PRO}}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{E}}\vdash t_{1}=t_{2}
where
t_{i}:r\to s
, then
\textbf{{PRO}}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{E}}\vdash K[t_{1}]=K[t_{2}]
for any contextualized term
K[\cdot]\in\langle\Sigma\rangle_{r,s}
.

The PROP freely generated by the set of generators
\Sigma
, denoted
\textbf{{PROP}}_{\Sigma}
, is defined as
\textbf{{PROP}}_{\Sigma}\coloneqq\textbf{{PRO}}_{\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma,\mathcal{E}_{0}}
where
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is the set containing
Equations

7
,
8
,
9
and
10
. That is, the terms are induced by the generators
\Sigma
as well as all symmetries
\Sigma_{0}
, and are quotiented by
Equations

1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
and
10
to form the equivalence class of
\textbf{{PROP}}_{\Sigma}
. Intuitively, compared to PROs, PROPs can bend the wires and gates can slide along those bends.

In this paper, we focus on two diagrammatic equivalence problems. For the first one (
Section

3
), we consider an arbitrary
state-and-effect-free
PRO, meaning that the set of generators is arbitrary but contains no states and no effects. In the second one (
Section

4
), we consider the PRO of permutations
\textbf{{PRO}}_{\Sigma_{0},\mathcal{E}_{0}}
, which is precisely the simplest PROP that one can think about, namely
\textbf{{PROP}}_{\varnothing}
.

2.3
Notations

A list of
p
elements of
A
is denoted
ks=[ks_{1},\dots,ks_{p}]\in A^{p}
. The list with
0
elements is denoted
[\,]\in A^{0}
. One can add
x\in A
to the left of
ks\in A^{p}
with the operator

\#

defined as
x\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ks\coloneqq[x,ks_{1},\ldots ks_{p}]\in A^{p+1}
. More generally, one can define the concatenation of two lists
ks\in A^{p}
and
\ell s\in A^{q}
with the operator
\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{{@}}}
defined as
ks\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{{@}}}\ell s\coloneqq[ks_{1},\ldots,ks_{p},\ell s_{1},\ldots\ell s_{q}]\in A^{p+q}
. For any
ks\in A^{p}
and
i\leq j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
, let
ks_{i\to j}\coloneqq[ks_{i},\dots,ks_{j}]\in A^{j-i+1}
be the sub-list of
ks
ranging from
i
to
j
(included). Notice that if
i>j
, then
ks_{i\to j}
is the empty list
[\,]
. If
(A,+)
is a group, given
ks\in A^{p}
and
x\in A
, we define
ks+_{\!\!\cdot}x\coloneqq[ks_{1}+x,\dots,ks_{q}+x]
and
ks-_{\!\!\cdot}x\coloneqq ks+_{\!\!\cdot}(-x)
.

Term rewriting systems (TRS) are defined as usual
[
2
]
. In particular, given two terms
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
, we write
t_{1}\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}t_{2}
when
t_{1}
can be rewritten into
t_{2}
using exactly one step of the rewriting system
\mathcal{R}
, and
t_{1}\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}t_{2}
when
t_{1}
can be rewritten into
t_{2}
using any finite number (including zero) of steps of
\mathcal{R}
. We write
t\,\not\!\!\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
when no rules of
\mathcal{R}
can be applied from the term
t
.

3
Term Rewriting in a State-and-effect-free PRO

In this section we fix
\Sigma
a state-and-effect-free set of generators, meaning that each generator
g\in\Sigma
satisfies
\textup{dom}(g)>0
and
\textup{cod}(g)>0
. This assumption enables clean termination and confluence results while remaining sufficiently general for a wide range practical applications. We consider
\textbf{{PRO}}_{\Sigma}
and solve its underlying diagrammatic equivalence problem: given two terms
t_{1},t_{2}\in\langle\Sigma\rangle
such that
\textbf{{PRO}}_{\Sigma}\vdash t_{1}=t_{2}
, how to effectively rewrite
t_{1}
into
t_{2}
using the coherence equations? To do so, we introduce a normal form and an associated rewriting system that we prove to be terminating and confluent.

3.1
Normal Form and Preprocessing

We define a
pre-normal form
as a sequence of layers, a layer being a single generator
g
sandwiched between identities:
L^{\smash{k}}_{\smash{\ell}}(g)\coloneqq\textup{id}_{k}\otimes(g\otimes\textup{id}_{\ell})
where
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
g\in\Sigma
. We say that
g
has
upper width

k
,
lower width

\ell
, and
height

k+\text{dom}(g)
.

A term
\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(gs)
, defined as follows, is a non-empty sequence of
p>0
layers, characterized by the list
gs\in\Sigma^{p}
of the associated generators and the lists
ks,\ell s\in\mathbb{N}^{p}
of their corresponding upper and lower widths.

\displaystyle\vec{L}^{\smash{k\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ks}}_{\smash{\ell\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}\ell s}}(g\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}gs)\coloneqq\begin{cases}L^{\smash{k}}_{\smash{\ell}}(g)&\text{if $ks=\ell s=gs=[\,]$}\\
L^{\smash{k}}_{\smash{\ell}}(g)\fatsemi\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(gs)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Notice that to guarantee that
\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(gs)
is well-defined, we assume throughout this paper that the three lists
ks
,
\ell s
and
gs
have the same size
p
and that
\forall i\in\{1,\dots,p-1\}
,
ks_{i}+\textup{cod}(gs_{i})+\ell s_{i}=ks_{i+1}+\textup{dom}(gs_{i+1})+\ell s_{i+1}
, to ensure that the layers have appropriate domains and codomains.

To obtain a
normal form
, we must fix an order for the commuting layers. To do so, we require the upper width of a generator to be smaller than the height of the next one, since the two layers can be commuted otherwise. This leads to the following definition of normal forms.

Definition 1
(normal form)

The set
\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{nf}}
of terms in
normal form
is inductively defined as follows:
\textup{id}_{n}
and
L^{\smash{k}}_{\smash{\ell}}(g)
are in normal form ; and
\vec{L}^{\smash{k\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ks}}_{\smash{\ell\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}\ell s}}(g\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}gs)
is in normal form if
\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(gs)
is in normal form while satisfying
k<ks_{1}+\textup{dom}(gs_{1})
.

Example 1

For instance, if
\Sigma=\{A:1\to 1,B:2\to 2\}
, the term
t=((A\fatsemi\textup{id}_{1})\otimes((A\otimes A)\fatsemi B))\fatsemi(B\otimes A)\in\langle\Sigma\rangle
is equivalent to
\vec{L}^{\smash{[0,1,2,1,0,2]}}_{\smash{[2,1,0,0,1,0]}}([A,A,A,B,B,A])
which is in normal from. These terms can be depicted as follows.

We introduce a procedure to turn any term into its normal form, relying on two new operators
\bullet
and
\star
allowing to compute the resulting normal form of a sequential and a parallel composition of two normal forms respectively. While the definition of
\star
is straightforward, the definition of
\bullet
requires mixing the layers of the two normal forms in order to satisfy the conditions explained in
Definition

1
.

Definition 2
(normalizing map)

Let
{\textup{NF}:\langle\Sigma\rangle\to\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{nf}}}
be the
normalizing map
that assigns to each term
t:n\to m
its normal form
\textup{NF}(t):n\to m
, inductively defined as

\displaystyle\textup{NF}(\textup{id}_{n})\coloneqq\textup{id}_{n}\hskip 30.00005pt\textup{NF}(g)\coloneqq L^{\smash{0}}_{\smash{0}}(g)

\displaystyle\textup{NF}(u\fatsemi v)\coloneqq\textup{NF}(u)\bullet\textup{NF}(v)\hskip 30.00005pt\textup{NF}(u\otimes v)\coloneqq\textup{NF}(u)\star\textup{NF}(v)

where
\bullet
and
\star
are inductively defined as follows, where
c=\textup{cod}(\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(gs))
and
d=\textup{dom}(\vec{L}^{\smash{rs}}_{\smash{ss}}(hs))
.

\displaystyle\textup{id}_{n}\bullet\textup{id}_{n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\textup{id}_{n}

\displaystyle\textup{id}_{n}\bullet\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(gs)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(gs)

\displaystyle\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(gs)\bullet\textup{id}_{n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(gs)

\displaystyle L^{\smash{k^{\prime}}}_{\smash{\ell^{\prime}}}(g^{\prime})\bullet\vec{L}^{\smash{k\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ks}}_{\smash{\ell\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}\ell s}}(g\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}gs)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}\vec{L}^{\smash{k^{\prime}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}k\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ks}}_{\smash{\ell^{\prime}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}\ell\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}\ell s}}(g^{\prime}\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}g\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}gs)\;\text{if $k^{\prime}<k+\textup{dom}(g)$}\\
L^{\smash{k}}_{\smash{\ell+\textup{def}(g^{\prime})}}(g)\fatsemi L^{\smash{k^{\prime}{-}\textup{def}(g)}}_{\smash{\ell^{\prime}}}(g^{\prime})\;\text{else if}\;ks=\ell s=gs=[\,]\\
L^{\smash{k}}_{\smash{\ell+\textup{def}(g^{\prime})}}(g)\fatsemi(L^{\smash{k^{\prime}{-}\textup{def}(g)}}_{\smash{\ell^{\prime}}}(g^{\prime})\bullet\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(gs))\;\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\vec{L}^{\smash{k\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ks}}_{\smash{\ell\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}\ell s}}(g\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}gs)\bullet\vec{L}^{\smash{rs}}_{\smash{ss}}(hs)

\displaystyle\coloneqq L^{\smash{k}}_{\smash{\ell}}(g)\bullet(\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(gs)\bullet\vec{L}^{\smash{rs}}_{\smash{ss}}(hs))

\displaystyle\textup{id}_{n}\star\textup{id}_{m}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\textup{id}_{n+m}

\displaystyle\textup{id}_{n}\star\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(gs)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\vec{L}^{\smash{ks+_{\!\!\cdot}n}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(gs)

\displaystyle\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(gs)\star\textup{id}_{n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s+_{\!\!\cdot}n}}(gs)

\displaystyle\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(gs)\star\vec{L}^{\smash{rs}}_{\smash{ss}}(hs)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\vec{L}^{\smash{ks\,\mathbin{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textup{@}$}}}\,(rs+_{\!\!\cdot}c)}}_{\smash{(\ell s+_{\!\!\cdot}d)\,\mathbin{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textup{@}$}}}\,ss}}(gs\mathbin{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{{@}}}hs)

Graphically, the notion of diagrammatic equivalence in PROs is known as
planar isotopy

[
20
, Theorem 3.1]
, and, roughly speaking, means that boxes can be moved horizontally without allowing wires to cross each other. To avoid graph-theoretic technicalities and keep the focus on term rewriting, we work under the assumption that the normalizing map
NF
yields exactly the notion of diagrammatic equivalence in PROs without showing that it relates to planar isotopy.

As stated in the following lemma, the normalizing map’s definition ensures that all normal forms are fix points, meaning that normalizing a normal form does not change the normal form.

Lemma 1

\textup{NF}(t)=t
for any
t\in\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{nf}}
.

Proof

The proof is by induction on the number of layers of
t
. The statement is true when
t=\textup{id}_{n}
and
t=L^{\smash{k}}_{\smash{\ell}}(g)
. Assume
\vec{L}^{\smash{k\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ks}}_{\smash{\ell\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}\ell s}}(g\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}gs)
is in normal form, so in particular
k<ks_{1}+\textup{dom}(gs_{1})
, which implies the following.

\textup{NF}(\vec{L}^{\smash{k\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ks}}_{\smash{\ell\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}\ell s}}(g\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}gs))=\textup{NF}(L^{\smash{k}}_{\smash{\ell}}(g))\bullet\textup{NF}(\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(gs))\\
\overset{\textup{IH}}{=}L^{\smash{k}}_{\smash{\ell}}(g)\bullet\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(gs)=\vec{L}^{\smash{k\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ks}}_{\smash{\ell\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}\ell s}}(g\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}gs)

This proof has been verified with Isabelle/HOL. ∎

In order to simplify the proofs on the TRS and make the correspondence between generators and layers clearer, we apply a preprocessing step to terms that replaces each generator
g
by its corresponding layer
L^{\smash{0}}_{\smash{0}}(g)
. The set of preprocessed terms is denoted
\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
and satisfies
\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{nf}}\subset\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{pp}}\subset\langle\Sigma\rangle
.

3.2
Terminating and Confluent Rewriting System

Let
\mathcal{R}
be the TRS containing the rewrite rules
R1
,
R2
,
R3
,
R4
,
R5
,
R6
,
R7
,
R8
,
R9
,
R10
and
R11
defined in
Figure

2
. Rewriting can be applied to sub-terms, meaning that
K[L]\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}K[R]
is a valid rewrite for any rewrite rule
(L\longrightarrow R)\in\mathcal{R}
and any
K[\cdot]\in\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\smash{\textup{pp}}}_{\smash{\textup{dom}(L),\textup{cod}(L)}}
. Moreover, some rewrite rules (
R7
,
R10
and
R11
) are subject to additional applicability conditions. All such conditions are decidable and stable under rewriting, ensuring that the normalization procedure is effective and suitable for automation. As stated in the following proposition, the rewriting system
\mathcal{R}
is sound with respect to the coherence equations.

Proposition 1

t_{1}\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}t_{2}\implies\textbf{{PRO}}_{\Sigma}\vdash t_{1}=t_{2}
for any
t_{1},t_{2}\in\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
.

Proof

For any rewrite rule
(L\longrightarrow R)\in\mathcal{R}
and any
K[\cdot]\in\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\smash{\textup{pp}}}_{\smash{\textup{dom}(L),\textup{cod}(L)}}
, we prove
\textbf{{PRO}}_{\Sigma}\vdash K[L]=K[R]
by induction on
K[\cdot]
where the only non-trivial case is
K[\cdot]=[\cdot]
. For most rewrite rules this is trivial, the only interesting cases
R10
and
R11
are proved using, in particular,
Equation

6
. ∎

(t\fatsemi u)\fatsemi v\longrightarrow t\fatsemi(u\fatsemi v)

(R1)

(t\otimes u)\otimes v\longrightarrow t\otimes(u\otimes v)

(R2)

\textup{id}_{n}\fatsemi t\longrightarrow t

(R3)

t\fatsemi\textup{id}_{n}\longrightarrow t

(R4)

\textup{id}_{n}\otimes\textup{id}_{m}\longrightarrow\textup{id}_{n+m}

(R5)

\textup{id}_{n}\otimes(\textup{id}_{m}\otimes t)\longrightarrow\textup{id}_{n+m}\otimes t

(R6)

u\otimes v\longrightarrow(u\otimes\textup{id}_{\textup{dom}(v)})\fatsemi(\textup{id}_{\textup{cod}(u)}\otimes v)\;\;\text{if}\;\;u\neq\textup{id}_{n}\wedge v\neq\textup{id}_{m}

(R7)

\textup{id}_{n}\otimes(u\fatsemi v)\longrightarrow(\textup{id}_{n}\otimes u)\fatsemi(\textup{id}_{n}\otimes v)

(R8)

(u\fatsemi v)\otimes\textup{id}_{n}\longrightarrow(u\otimes\textup{id}_{n})\fatsemi(v\otimes\textup{id}_{n})

(R9)

\begin{array}[]{c}L^{\smash{k_{1}}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(g_{1})\fatsemi L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}}}(g_{2})\longrightarrow L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}+\textup{def}(g_{1})}}(g_{2})\fatsemi L^{\smash{k_{1}-\textup{def}(g_{2})}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(g_{1})\\
\text{if}\;\;k_{1}\geq k_{2}+\textup{dom}(g_{2})\end{array}

(R10)

\begin{array}[]{c}L^{\smash{k_{1}}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(g_{1})\fatsemi(L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}}}(g_{2})\fatsemi t)\longrightarrow L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}+\textup{def}(g_{1})}}(g_{2})\fatsemi(L^{\smash{k_{1}-\textup{def}(g_{2})}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(g_{1})\fatsemi t)\\
\text{if}\;\;k_{1}\geq k_{2}+\textup{dom}(g_{2})\end{array}

(R11)

Figure 2
:
The rewrite rules are defined for any
n,m,k_{1},k_{2},\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
,
g_{1},g_{2}\in\Sigma
,
t,u,v\in\langle\Sigma\rangle
and
R7
,
R10
and
R11
are conditioned.

In the following, we prove that preprocessed terms are reduced into their normal form by applying
\mathcal{R}
. To do so, we show that
\mathcal{R}
terminates and is confluent. We initially attempted to rely on automated termination and confluence provers such as
AProVE

[
14
]
and the provers of the
CoCo
competition
[
16
]
. While these tools were useful for guiding early refinements of the rules, they ultimately did not succeed in producing termination or confluence certificates for our system (at least used from their web interface by our non-expert hands). We therefore carried out the proofs manually and formalize the most important steps with the proof assistant Isabelle/HOL. To keep the formalization light weight, we chose not to use the term rewriting infrastructure present in Isabelle/HOL, concentrating instead our formalizing efforts on the equalities and inequalities at the heart of the proofs.

Proposition 2

\mathcal{R}
terminates.

Proof

We show that any rewrite sequence
t_{1}\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}t_{2}\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}t_{3}\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}\dots
is finite. To do so, we define two positive quantities
\beta:\langle\Sigma\rangle\to\mathbb{N}
and
\delta_{D}:\langle\Sigma\rangle\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
(see
Figure

3
), where the former is parameterized by
D\in\mathbb{N}
, and prove that
(\beta,\delta_{D})
strictly decreases by lexicographical order, for a carefully chosen
D\in\mathbb{N}
.
Notice that as
\delta_{D}
takes real positive value, we must also show that it decreases by at least a constant at each step, otherwise the value could decrease infinitely.
Given a term
t\in\langle\Sigma\rangle
, let
D(t)\coloneqq 2+\max_{g\in\textup{gen}(t)}|\textup{def}(g)|
. Notice that
D(\cdot)
is preserved under rewriting, meaning that
D(t_{i})=D(t_{j})
for any
i,j
. We prove that

\displaystyle\beta(K[L])>\beta(K[R])\quad\text{or}\quad\beta(K[L])=\beta(K[R])\wedge\delta_{D}(K[L])\geq\delta_{D}(K[R])+\nicefrac{{1}}{{D}}

for any rewrite rule
(L\longrightarrow R)\in\mathcal{R}
and any context
K[\cdot]\in\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\smash{\textup{pp}}}_{\smash{\textup{dom}(L),\textup{cod}(L)}}
, where we chose
D\coloneqq D(K[L])=D(K[R])
. This is straightforwardly proved by induction on
K[\cdot]
where the base cases
K[\cdot]=[\cdot]
have been verified with Isabelle/HOL. More precisely, we prove that
R1
,
R2
,
R3
,
R4
,
R5
,
R6
,
R7
,
R8
and
R9
strictly decrease
\beta
while
R10
and
R11
preserve
\beta
and strictly decrease
\delta_{D}
. ∎

Remark 1

The fact that
R10
and
R11
strictly decrease
\delta_{D}
is not trivial, and is only true with a careful choice of
D\in\mathbb{N}
. More precisely,
D\coloneqq D(K[L])=D(K[R])
ensures that
D\geq 2-\textup{def}(g_{2})
, which implies
1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{D}}\geq\nicefrac{{1}}{{D}}(1-\textup{def}(g_{2}))
. Moreover, the condition
k_{1}\geq k_{2}+\textup{dom}(g_{2})
with
\textup{dom}(g_{2})\geq 1
yields the following.

\displaystyle k_{1}\geq k_{2}+1\implies(1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{D}})k_{1}\geq(1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{D}})(k_{2}+1)

\displaystyle\implies k_{1}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{D}}k_{2}\geq k_{2}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{D}}k_{1}+(1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{D}})\geq k_{2}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{D}}k_{1}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{D}}(1-\textup{def}(g_{2}))

\displaystyle\implies k_{1}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{D}}k_{2}\geq k_{2}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{D}}(k_{1}-\textup{def}(g_{2}))+\nicefrac{{1}}{{D}}\implies\delta_{D}(L)\geq\delta_{D}(R)+\nicefrac{{1}}{{D}}

Lemma 2

t_{1}\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}t_{2}{\implies}\textup{NF}(t_{1})=\textup{NF}(t_{2})
for any
t_{1},t_{2}\in\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
.

Proof

For any rewrite rule
(L\longrightarrow R)\in\mathcal{R}
and any
K[\cdot]\in\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\smash{\textup{pp}}}_{\smash{\textup{dom}(L),\textup{cod}(L)}}
, the equality
\textup{NF}(K[L])=\textup{NF}(K[R])
is straightforwardly proved by induction on
K[\cdot]
where the base cases
K[\cdot]=[\cdot]
have been verified with Isabelle/HOL. ∎

\alpha:\langle\Sigma\rangle\to\mathbb{N}

\beta:\langle\Sigma\rangle\to\mathbb{N}

\gamma:\langle\Sigma\rangle\to\mathbb{N}

\delta_{D}:\langle\Sigma\rangle\to\mathbb{R}^{+}

Figure 3
:
The top table defines the positive quantities
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta_{D}
, where the former is parameterized by
D\in\mathbb{N}
. These quantities are used to prove the termination of
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{R}_{0}
in

and

respectively. The last line of the table gives the value of each quantity applied to layers. The bottom table describes which rewrite rules strictly decrease (

) each quantity. All cases so indicated have been verified with Isabelle/HOL.

Lemma 3

t\,\not\!\!\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}\implies t\in\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{nf}}
for any
t\in\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
.

Proof

By induction on
t
. The base cases
t=\textup{id}_{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
are direct because
\textup{id}_{n}\,\not\!\!\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
and
\textup{id}_{n}\in\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{nf}}
. The base cases
t\in\Sigma
are trivial because
t\notin\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
.

•

Suppose
t=u\fatsemi v
and
t\,\not\!\!\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
. Then
u,v\in\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
and
u\,\not\!\!\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}},v\,\not\!\!\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
which, by IH, implies
u,v\in\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{nf}}
. Notice that
u\neq\textup{id}_{n}\neq v
otherwise
R3
or
R4
applies. So, it must be the case that
u=L^{\smash{k}}_{\smash{\ell}}(g)
and
v=\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(gs)
for some
k,\ell,p\in\mathbb{N}
,
g\in\Sigma
,
ks,\ell s\in\mathbb{N}^{p}
,
gs\in\Sigma^{p}
such that
p>0
, otherwise (
R1
) applies. Moreover,
k<ks_{1}+\textup{dom}(gs_{1})
, otherwise
R10
or
R11
applies. Hence,
t=\vec{L}^{\smash{k\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ks}}_{\smash{\ell\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}\ell s}}(g\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}gs)\in\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{nf}}
.

•

Suppose
t=u\otimes v
and
t\,\not\!\!\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
. Then
u\,\not\!\!\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}},v\,\not\!\!\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
. Notice that
u
and
v
are not necessarily in
\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
, so we cannot use the IH. Nevertheless, we know that
u,v\notin\Sigma
, otherwise
t\notin\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
. In fact, we know for sure that
u=\textup{id}_{k}
and
v=v_{1}\otimes v_{2}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
v_{1},v_{2}\in\langle\Sigma\rangle
, otherwise a rule among
R2
,
R5
,
R7
,
R8
and
R9
applies. For the same reason and the fact that (
R6
) is not applicable, it must be the case that
v_{1}=g
and
v_{2}=\textup{id}_{\ell}
for some
g\in\Sigma
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence,
t=\textup{id}_{k}\otimes(g\otimes\textup{id}_{\ell})=L^{\smash{k}}_{\smash{\ell}}(g)\in\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{nf}}
.

∎

Proposition 3

\mathcal{R}
is confluent in
\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
.

Proof

Let
t\in\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
. If
t\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}u
and
t\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}v
, then by
Proposition

2
there exist
u^{\prime}v^{\prime}\in\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
such that
u\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}u^{\prime}\,\not\!\!\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
and
v\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}v^{\prime}\,\not\!\!\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
.
Lemmas

1
,
2
and
3
imply
\textup{NF}(t)=u^{\prime}=v^{\prime}
, which concludes the proof. ∎

As stated in the following theorem, the termination and confluence of
\mathcal{R}
solve our problem: given two diagrammatically equivalence terms
t_{1},t_{2}\in\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
, we can effectively rewrite
t_{1}
into
t_{2}
using the coherence equations.

Theorem 3.1

\textup{NF}(t_{1})=\textup{NF}(t_{2})\implies t_{1}\longleftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}t_{2}
for any
t_{1},t_{2}\in\langle\Sigma\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
.

Proof

This is a direct consequence of
Propositions

2
and
3
.∎

While the rewrite rules can be applied in any order, it may be more efficient to follow a predefined rewriting strategy. In particular,
R1
,
R2
,
R3
,
R4
,
R5
,
R6
,
R7
,
R8
and
R9
allow transforming any preprocessed term into a pre-normal form, and
R10
and
R11
allow transforming the layers to fulfill the normal form conditions. Hence, the two steps can be applied one after the other.

4
Term Rewriting in the PRO of Permutations

In this section, we consider the PRO of permutations
\textbf{{PRO}}_{\Sigma_{0},\mathcal{E}_{0}}
, whose generators are the symmetries, i.e.
\Sigma_{0}\coloneqq\{\sigma_{n,m}:n,m\in\mathbb{N}\}
and where
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is the set containing
Equations

7
,
8
,
9
and
10
.
\textbf{{PRO}}_{\Sigma_{0},\mathcal{E}_{0}}
is the simplest PROP that one can think about, namely
\textbf{{PROP}}_{\varnothing}
. Intuitively, in this structure, the diagrams have no boxes and are just made of wires, which allows expressing any permutation. Notice that all terms in
\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle
have an equal number of inputs and outputs. We solve the underlying diagrammatic equivalence problem: given two terms
t_{1},t_{2}\in\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle
expressing the same permutation, how to effectively rewrite
t_{1}
into
t_{2}
using the coherence equations (
Equations

1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
and
10
)? To do so, we refine the normal form of
Definition

1
to define a normal form for the PRO of permutations—that we call
canonical form
to prevent confusion—and provide an associated rewriting system that we prove to be terminating and confluent.

4.1
Canonical Form and Interpretation

The permutations of size
n\in\mathbb{N}
, whose set is denoted
\textbf{{Perm}}(n)
, are the bijective maps from
\{1,\dots,n\}
to itself. A permutation
{\pi\in\textbf{{Perm}}(n)}
is denoted
(\pi(1),\dots,\pi(n))
. For instance,
{(2,1,3)\in\textbf{{Perm}}(3)}
is the permutation mapping
1
to
2
,
2
to
1
and
3
to itself. There is only one permutation of size
0
, denoted
(\,)
, and only one permutation of size
1
, denoted
(1)
. In the following,
|\pi|
denotes the size of the permutation
\pi
and
\pi^{-1}
its inverse. Two permutations
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\in\textbf{{Perm}}(n)
can be composed sequentially with
\circ
as the usual composition, i.e.
(\pi_{2}\circ\pi_{1})(i)\coloneqq\pi_{2}(\pi_{1}(i))
for any
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Two permutations
\pi_{1}\in\textbf{{Perm}}(n),\pi_{2}\in\textbf{{Perm}}(m)
can be composed in parallel with the direct sum
\oplus
defined as follows for any
i\in\{1,\dots,n+m\}
.

\displaystyle(\pi_{1}\oplus\pi_{2})(i)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\pi_{1}(i)&\text{if}\;1\leq i\leq n\\
n+\pi_{2}(i-n)&\text{if}\;n<i\leq n+m\end{cases}

Let
\textbf{{Perm}}\coloneqq\cup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\textbf{{Perm}}(n)
be the collection of all permutations. Then, each term
t:n\to n\in\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle
can be interpreted as a permutation of size
n
.

Definition 3
(interpretation)

Let
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket:\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle\to\textbf{{Perm}}
be the
interpretation
, inductively defined as follows.

\displaystyle\llbracket\textup{id}_{n}\rrbracket\coloneqq(1,\dots,n)\hskip 30.00005pt\llbracket\sigma_{n,m}\rrbracket\coloneqq(n+1,\dots,n+m,1,\dots,n)

\displaystyle\llbracket u\fatsemi v\rrbracket\coloneqq\llbracket v\rrbracket\circ\llbracket u\rrbracket\hskip 30.00005pt\llbracket u\otimes v\rrbracket\coloneqq\llbracket u\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket v\rrbracket

Given
d\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\tau_{d}\coloneqq\sigma_{d-1,1}
be the
toboggan
of size
d
. Intuitively, the diagrammatic representation of
\tau_{d}
lifts its last wire to the top, and its interpretation is
\llbracket\tau_{d}\rrbracket=(2,\dots,d,1)
, which implies the following equality.

\displaystyle\llbracket L^{\smash{k}}_{\smash{\ell}}(\tau_{d})\rrbracket=(1,\dots,k,k+2,\dots,k+d,k+1,k+d+1,\dots,k+d+\ell)

We now define the
canonical form
and its associated canonizing map. Given
ks,\ell s,ds\in\mathbb{N}^{p}
with
p>0
, we introduce the following notation.

\displaystyle\vec{C}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(ds)\coloneqq\vec{L}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}([\tau_{ds_{i}}]_{i\in[p]})

Definition 4
(canonical form)

The set
\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\textup{cf}}
of terms in
canonical form
is inductively defined as follows:
\textup{id}_{n}
is in canonical form ;
L^{\smash{k}}_{\smash{\ell}}(\tau_{d})
is in canonical form if
d\geq 2
; and
\vec{C}^{\smash{k\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ks}}_{\smash{\ell\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}\ell s}}(d\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ds)
is in canonical form if
d\geq 2
and
\vec{C}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(ds)
is in canonical form while satisfying
k<ks_{1}
.

Example 2

The term
(\textup{id}_{1}\otimes\tau_{2}\otimes\tau_{2})\fatsemi(\sigma_{2,2}\otimes\textup{id}_{1})\fatsemi(\sigma_{1,2}\otimes\tau_{2})\fatsemi(\textup{id}_{1}\otimes\sigma_{1,2}\otimes\textup{id}_{1})\fatsemi(\textup{id}_{3}\otimes\tau_{2})\fatsemi(\tau_{4}\otimes\textup{id}_{1})
is equivalent to
\vec{C}^{\smash{[0,1,2,3]}}_{\smash{[2,0,1,0]}}([3,4,2,2])
, which is in canonical form. These terms can be graphically depicted as follows.

Definition 5
(canonizing map)

Let
\textup{CF}:\textbf{{Perm}}\to\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\textup{cf}}
be the
canonizing map
that assigns to each permutation
\pi\in\textbf{{Perm}}(n)
its term in canonical form
\textup{CF}(\pi):n\to n
, inductively defined as

\displaystyle\textup{CF}((\;))\coloneqq\textup{id}_{0}\hskip 30.00005pt\textup{CF}((1))\coloneqq\textup{id}_{1}

\displaystyle\textup{CF}(\pi)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\textup{id}_{1}\star\textup{CF}(\pi|_{1})&\text{if}\;\pi(1)=1\\
L^{\smash{0}}_{\smash{|\pi|-\pi^{-1}(1)}}(\tau_{\pi^{-1}(1)})\bullet(\textup{id}_{1}\star\textup{CF}(\pi|_{1}))&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

where
\pi|_{1}\in\textbf{{Perm}}(|\pi|-1)
is the permutation satisfying
(\pi|_{1})(i)\coloneqq\pi(i)-1
when
1\leq i<\pi^{-1}(1)
and
(\pi|_{1})(i)\coloneqq\pi(i+1)-1
when
\pi^{-1}(1)\leq i\leq|\pi|-1
.

Notice that
\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\textup{cf}}\subset\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\textup{nf}}\subset\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\textup{pp}}\subset\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle
. Moreover, as stated in the following lemma, the canonizing map’s definition is compatible with
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
.

Lemma 4

\textup{CF}(\llbracket t\rrbracket)=t
for any
t\in\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\textup{cf}}
.

Proof

In the following we use the notation
\pi^{\oplus n}\coloneqq\pi\oplus\dots\oplus\pi
(with
n
compositions
\oplus
).
If
t=\textup{id}_{n}
, then
\textup{CF}(\llbracket\textup{id}_{n}\rrbracket)=\textup{CF}((1)^{\oplus n})=\textup{id}_{n}
. Otherwise, we proceed by induction on the number of layers. If
t=L^{\smash{k}}_{\smash{\ell}}(\tau_{d})
, then
\textup{CF}(\llbracket L^{\smash{k}}_{\smash{\ell}}(\tau_{d})\rrbracket)=\textup{id}_{k}\star\textup{CF}(\llbracket L^{\smash{0}}_{\smash{\ell}}(\tau_{d})\rrbracket)=\textup{id}_{k}\star(L^{\smash{0}}_{\smash{\ell}}(\tau_{d})\bullet(\textup{id}_{1}\star\textup{CF}((1)^{\oplus\ell+d-1})))=L^{\smash{k}}_{\smash{\ell}}(\tau_{d})
. Moreover, if
t=\vec{C}^{\smash{k\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ks}}_{\smash{\ell\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}\ell s}}(d\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ds)
, then we have the following proof.

\displaystyle\textup{CF}(\llbracket C(k\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ks,\ell\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}\ell s,d\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ds)\rrbracket)=\textup{id}_{k}\star\textup{CF}(\llbracket C(0\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}(ks-_{\!\!\cdot}k),\ell\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}\ell s,d\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ds)\rrbracket)

\displaystyle=\textup{id}_{k}\star(L^{\smash{0}}_{\smash{\ell+d-d}}(\tau_{d})\bullet(\textup{id}_{1}\star\textup{CF}(\llbracket C(ks-_{\!\!\cdot}k-_{\!\!\cdot}1,\ell s,ds)\rrbracket)))

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{IH}}{=}\textup{id}_{k}\star(L^{\smash{0}}_{\smash{\ell}}(\tau_{d})\bullet(\textup{id}_{1}\star C(ks-_{\!\!\cdot}k-_{\!\!\cdot}1,\ell s,ds)))=C(k\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ks,\ell\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}\ell s,d\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.6}{$\#$}}ds)

∎

4.2
Terminating and Confluent Rewriting System

We introduce the new rewrite rules in
Figure

4
. The rules
R1
,
R2
,
R3
,
R4
,
R5
,
R6
,
R7
,
R8
and
R9
(see
Figure

2
) together with the rules
R12
,
R13
,
R14
,
R15
,
R17
,
R19
,
R21
,
R16
,
R18
,
R20
and
R22
form the rewriting system
\mathcal{R}_{0}
. Notice that
\mathcal{R}_{0}
contains all rules of
\mathcal{R}
except
R10
and
R11
, which have been replaced by the rules
R15
,
R17
,
R19
,
R21
,
R16
,
R18
,
R20
and
R22
. As stated in the following proposition, the rewriting system
\mathcal{R}_{0}
is sound with respect to the coherence equations.

Proposition 4

t_{1}\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}^{*}t_{2}\implies\textbf{{PRO}}_{\Sigma_{0},\mathcal{E}_{0}}\vdash t_{1}=t_{2}
for any
t_{1},t_{2}\in\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
.

Proof

For any rewrite rule
(L\longrightarrow R)\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
and any
K[\cdot]\in\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\smash{\textup{pp}}}_{\smash{\textup{dom}(L),\textup{cod}(L)}}
, we prove
\textbf{{PRO}}_{\Sigma_{0},\mathcal{E}_{0}}\vdash K[L]=K[R]
by induction on
K[\cdot]
where the only non-trivial case is
K[\cdot]=[\cdot]
. For most rewrite rules this is trivial, the only interesting cases
R14
,
R15
,
R17
,
R19
,
R21
,
R16
,
R18
,
R20
and
R22
are proved using, in particular,
Equations

8
,
9
and
10
. ∎

\sigma_{0,n}\longrightarrow\textup{id}_{n}

(R12)

\sigma_{n,0}\longrightarrow\textup{id}_{n}

(R13)

\sigma_{n+1,m+2}\longrightarrow(\sigma_{n+1,m+1}\otimes\textup{id}_{1})\fatsemi(\textup{id}_{m+1}\otimes\tau_{n+2})

(R14)

\begin{array}[]{c}L^{\smash{k_{1}}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(\tau_{d_{1}})\fatsemi L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}}}(\tau_{d_{2}})\longrightarrow L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}}}(\tau_{d_{2}})\fatsemi L^{\smash{k_{1}+1}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}-1}}(\tau_{d_{1}})\\
\text{if}\;\;d_{1}\geq 2,d_{2}\geq 2\;\;\text{and}\;\;k_{2}\leq k_{1}<k_{2}+d_{2}-d_{1}\end{array}

(R15)

\begin{array}[]{c}L^{\smash{k_{1}}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(\tau_{d_{1}})\fatsemi(L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}}}(\tau_{d_{2}})\fatsemi t)\longrightarrow L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}}}(\tau_{d_{2}})\fatsemi(L^{\smash{k_{1}+1}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}-1}}(\tau_{d_{1}})\fatsemi t)\\
\text{if}\;\;d_{1}\geq 2,d_{2}\geq 2\;\;\text{and}\;\;k_{2}\leq k_{1}<k_{2}+d_{2}-d_{1}\end{array}

(R16)

\begin{array}[]{c}L^{\smash{k_{1}}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(\tau_{d_{1}})\fatsemi L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}}}(\tau_{d_{2}})\longrightarrow L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}+1}}(\tau_{d_{2}-1})\fatsemi L^{\smash{k_{1}+1}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(\tau_{d_{1}-1})\\
\text{if}\;\;d_{1}\geq 2,d_{2}\geq 2\;\;\text{and}\;\;k_{2}\leq k_{1}\wedge k_{2}+d_{2}-d_{1}\leq k_{1}<k_{2}+d_{2}-1\end{array}

(R17)

\begin{array}[]{c}L^{\smash{k_{1}}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(\tau_{d_{1}})\fatsemi(L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}}}(\tau_{d_{2}})\fatsemi t)\longrightarrow L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}+1}}(\tau_{d_{2}-1})\fatsemi(L^{\smash{k_{1}+1}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(\tau_{d_{1}-1})\fatsemi t)\\
\text{if}\;\;d_{1}\geq 2,d_{2}\geq 2\;\;\text{and}\;\;k_{2}\leq k_{1}\wedge k_{2}+d_{2}-d_{1}\leq k_{1}<k_{2}+d_{2}-1\end{array}

(R18)

\begin{array}[]{c}L^{\smash{k_{1}}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(\tau_{d_{1}})\fatsemi L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}}}(\tau_{d_{2}})\longrightarrow L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(\tau_{d_{1}+d_{2}-1})\\
\text{if}\;\;d_{1}\geq 2,d_{2}\geq 2\;\;\text{and}\;\;k_{1}=k_{2}+d_{2}-1\end{array}

(R19)

\begin{array}[]{c}L^{\smash{k_{1}}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(\tau_{d_{1}})\fatsemi(L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}}}(\tau_{d_{2}})\fatsemi t)\longrightarrow L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(\tau_{d_{1}+d_{2}-1})\fatsemi t\\
\text{if}\;\;d_{1}\geq 2,d_{2}\geq 2\;\;\text{and}\;\;k_{1}=k_{2}+d_{2}-1\end{array}

(R20)

\begin{array}[]{c}L^{\smash{k_{1}}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(\tau_{d_{1}})\fatsemi L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}}}(\tau_{d_{2}})\longrightarrow L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}}}(\tau_{d_{2}})\fatsemi L^{\smash{k_{1}}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(\tau_{d_{1}})\\
\text{if}\;\;d_{1}\geq 2,d_{2}\geq 2\;\;\text{and}\;\;k_{1}>k_{2}+d_{2}-1\end{array}

(R21)

\begin{array}[]{c}L^{\smash{k_{1}}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(\tau_{d_{1}})\fatsemi(L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}}}(\tau_{d_{2}})\fatsemi t)\longrightarrow L^{\smash{k_{1}}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(\tau_{d_{1}})\fatsemi(L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}}}(\tau_{d_{2}})\fatsemi t)\\
\text{if}\;\;d_{1}\geq 2,d_{2}\geq 2\;\;\text{and}\;\;k_{1}>k_{2}+d_{2}-1\end{array}

(R22)

Figure 4
:
The rewrite rules are defined for any
n,m,k_{1},k_{2},\ell_{1},\ell_{2},d_{1},d_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
and
R15
,
R17
,
R19
,
R21
,
R16
,
R18
,
R20
and
R22
are conditioned.

Remark 2

R15
,
R17
,
R19
and
R21
explain how to transform a sequence of two layers
L^{\smash{k_{1}}}_{\smash{\ell_{1}}}(\tau_{d_{1}})
and
L^{\smash{k_{2}}}_{\smash{\ell_{2}}}(\tau_{d_{2}})
whenever
k_{1}\geq k_{2}
, and
R16
,
R18
,
R20
and
R22
apply the same transformations when more layers follow. Here are some graphical examples.

In order to prove termination and confluence, we build upon the result of
Section

3
and follow a similar proof flow.

Proposition 5

\mathcal{R}_{0}
terminates.

Proof

We build upon the proof of
Proposition

2
by defining two additional positive quantities
\alpha,\gamma:\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle\to\mathbb{N}
and show that
\mathcal{R}_{0}
strictly decreases
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta_{2})
with lexicographical order (notice that, as
\textup{def}(g)=0
for any
g\in\Sigma_{0}
, we can take
D=2
for
\delta_{D}
). The definitions of
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
and
\delta_{2}
are given in
Figure

3
together with a sum up of which rewrite rules strictly decrease or preserve each quantity. Again, all the base cases have been verified with Isabelle/HOL. ∎

Lemma 5

t_{1}\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}^{*}t_{2}\implies\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket=\llbracket t_{2}\rrbracket
for any
t_{1},t_{2}\in\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\textup{pp}}

Proof

For any rewrite rule
(L\longrightarrow R)\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
and any
K[\cdot]\in\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\smash{\textup{pp}}}_{\smash{\textup{dom}(L),\textup{cod}(L)}}
, the equality
\llbracket K[L]\rrbracket=\llbracket K[R]\rrbracket
is straightforwardly proved by induction on
K[\cdot]
where the base cases
K[\cdot]=[\cdot]
have been verified with Isabelle/HOL.∎

Lemma 6

t\,\not\!\!\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}\implies t\in\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\textup{cf}}
for any
t\in\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
.

Proof

All rewrite rules of
\mathcal{R}
are in
\mathcal{R}_{0}
except
R10
and
R11
, which exactly correspond to
R21
and
R22
in
\mathcal{R}_{0}
when
g_{i}=\tau_{d_{i}}
for some
d_{i}\geq 2
. Moreover, every generator
\sigma_{n,m}
in
t
satisfies
n>0
and
m=1
, otherwise a rule among
R12
,
R13
and
R14
applies. Thus,
Lemma

3
applies and we can assume that
t\in\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\textup{nf}}
. This implies that there exists
ks,\ell s,ds\in\mathbb{N}^{p}
for some
p>0
satisfying
ds_{i}\geq 2
for any
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
, such that
t=\vec{C}^{\smash{ks}}_{\smash{\ell s}}(ds)
. But then
\forall i\in\{1,\dots,p-1\}:ks_{i}<ks_{i+1}
, otherwise a rule among
R15
,
R16
,
R17
,
R18
,
R19
and
R20
applies. Hence,
t\in\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\textup{cf}}
. ∎

Proposition 6

\mathcal{R}_{0}
is confluent in
\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
.

Proof

Let
t\in\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
. If
t\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}^{*}u
and
t\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}^{*}v
, then by
Proposition

5
there exist
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
such that
u\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}^{*}u^{\prime}\,\not\!\!\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}
and
v\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}^{*}v^{\prime}\,\not\!\!\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}
.
Lemmas

4
,
5
and
6
imply
\textup{CF}(\llbracket t\rrbracket)=u^{\prime}=v^{\prime}
, which concludes the proof. ∎

As stated in the following theorem, the termination and confluence of
\mathcal{R}_{0}
solve our problem: given two terms
t_{1},t_{2}\in\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
expressing the same permutation, we can effectively rewrite
t_{1}
into
t_{2}
using the coherence equations.

Theorem 4.1

\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket=\llbracket t_{2}\rrbracket\implies t_{1}\longleftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{0}}^{*}t_{2}
for any
t_{1},t_{2}\in\langle\Sigma_{0}\rangle^{\textup{pp}}
.

Proof

This is a direct consequence of
Propositions

5
and
6
.∎

5
Conclusion

In this work, we addressed the problem of diagrammatic equivalence for two classes of diagrams, namely
\textbf{{PRO}}_{\Sigma}
(for an arbitrary state-and-effect-free set of generators
\Sigma
) and
\textbf{{PRO}}_{\Sigma_{0},\mathcal{E}_{0}}=\textbf{{PROP}}_{\varnothing}
, which can express any permutation. We used a term rewriting approach, providing terminating and confluent TRSs. Our plan is to combine our solutions to design a rewriting-based approach to handle diagrammatic equivalences in an arbitrary
\textbf{{PROP}}_{\Sigma}
. It would also be interesting to generalize the first TRS to arbitrary PROs (not necessarily state-and-effect-free). Also, we could leverage the existing term-rewriting infrastructure present in Isabelle/HOL
[
23
]
to obtain a verified checker for our TRSs, or create proof-checkers that compute the normal/canonical forms in a verified way following Sternagel and Thiemann
[
22
]
. Our main objective—initiated by this first contribution—is to obtain a fully verified pipeline for certifying equivalences of quantum circuits.
